People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I haven’t really noticed repeated thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, in order to feel right or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s to prevent something. It just… happens. Like I’ll tap the scanner in a pattern or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and it doesn’t really fit what I’m supposed to be doing. Then the customer’s waiting, and I can’t move on right away. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to tap the scanner in a certain way or freeze with your hand over the cash drawer because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “compelled” is the right word. It’s more like I get stuck doing it… tapping the scanner or just freezing there with my hand over the cash drawer, even though the customer’s waiting and I should move on. It’s been enough of a problem that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you get stuck doing these actions at the register?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess distressed, mostly because it’s embarrassing. I’m standing there tapping or frozen over the cash drawer and the customer’s just waiting. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing, and then I got written up last week, so… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't tap the scanner in that certain way or if you moved your hand from the cash drawer without freezing for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not like something bad would happen. I just get stuck. The tapping, or my hand staying over the cash drawer… it doesn’t make much sense, and the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing, and then I got written up last week, so… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't tap the scanner in that certain way or if you moved your hand from the cash drawer without freezing for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not like something bad would happen. I just get stuck. The tapping, or my hand staying over the cash drawer… it doesn’t make much sense, and the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things in a certain order or in a symmetrical way when you're at the register?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… I get stuck in a pattern, like tapping the scanner, or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer when I should be helping the customer. It doesn’t really feel like an order thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about germs or contamination when you're working at the register?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about germs. I mean, I handle money and stuff, but that’s not really what I’m worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't do these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, you would make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. I don’t think, “If I don’t tap, I’ll make a mistake.” It’s more like I just… get caught doing it, or I freeze, and then I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. The customer’s waiting, and last week I got written up for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… work, mostly. I’m a cashier, and I’ve been… getting stuck doing things at the register that don’t really make sense. Like tapping the scanner a certain way, or just freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while someone’s waiting. It happened enough that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing these kinds of repetitive actions or getting stuck when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really that I’ve noticed. It’s mostly at work, at the register. I get stuck tapping the scanner a certain way, or I freeze with my hand over the drawer while somebody’s waiting. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes happen at the register?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly anxious. I mean, I’m aware people are waiting, so that’s uncomfortable, but I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think I have thoughts looping like that. It’s more just… I get stuck in the moment. I haven’t really noticed repeated thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, in order to feel right or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s to prevent something. It just… happens. Like I’ll tap the scanner in a pattern or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and it doesn’t really fit what I’m supposed to be doing. Then the customer’s waiting, and I can’t move on right away. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to tap the scanner in a certain way or freeze with your hand over the cash drawer because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “compelled” is the right word. It’s more like I get stuck doing it… tapping the scanner or just freezing there with my hand over the cash drawer, even though the customer’s waiting and I should move on. It’s been enough of a problem that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you get stuck doing these actions at the register?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess distressed, mostly because it’s embarrassing. I’m standing there tapping or frozen over the cash drawer and the customer’s just waiting. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing, and then I got written up last week, so… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't tap the scanner in that certain way or if you moved your hand from the cash drawer without freezing for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not like something bad would happen. I just get stuck. The tapping, or my hand staying over the cash drawer… it doesn’t make much sense, and the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things in a certain order or in a symmetrical way when you're at the register?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… I get stuck in a pattern, like tapping the scanner, or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer when I should be helping the customer. It doesn’t really feel like an order thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about germs or contamination when you're working at the register?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about germs. I mean, I handle money and stuff, but that’s not really what I’m worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't do these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, you would make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. I don’t think, “If I don’t tap, I’ll make a mistake.” It’s more like I just… get caught doing it, or I freeze, and then I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. The customer’s waiting, and last week I got written up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would lead to something going wrong with the transaction?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think something would go wrong with the transaction because I didn’t do it. It’s more that I get stuck doing it, or I freeze there with my hand over the cash drawer, and then the customer’s just waiting. That’s what got me in trouble last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these actions at the register would lead to you making an error with the customer's order?\n\nPatient: No… not really an error with the order. It’s more that I get stuck doing those things, like tapping the scanner or just freezing by the cash drawer, and then I’m not moving the line along. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would make you feel like you've made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’ve made a mistake, no. It’s more… I get caught up in the tapping or I just stop with my hand over the drawer, and then the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that doing these specific actions at the register helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it helps me avoid mistakes. It’s just something I get stuck doing, like tapping the scanner or stopping over the cash drawer, and then the customer’s waiting. That’s what caused the trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these actions at the register is related to trying to do your job perfectly?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. I just sort of get stuck there, tapping the scanner or holding still by the drawer, even when the customer’s waiting. It happened enough that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing these kinds of repetitive actions or getting stuck when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really that I’ve noticed. It’s mostly at work, at the register. I get stuck tapping the scanner a certain way, or I freeze with my hand over the drawer while somebody’s waiting. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes happen at the register?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly anxious. I mean, I’m aware people are waiting, so that’s uncomfortable, but I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think I have thoughts looping like that. It’s more just… I get stuck in the moment. I haven’t really noticed repeated thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, in order to feel right or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s to prevent something. It just… happens. Like I’ll tap the scanner in a pattern or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and it doesn’t really fit what I’m supposed to be doing. Then the customer’s waiting, and I can’t move on right away. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to tap the scanner in a certain way or freeze with your hand over the cash drawer because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “compelled” is the right word. It’s more like I get stuck doing it… tapping the scanner or just freezing there with my hand over the cash drawer, even though the customer’s waiting and I should move on. It’s been enough of a problem that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you get stuck doing these actions at the register?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess distressed, mostly because it’s embarrassing. I’m standing there tapping or frozen over the cash drawer and the customer’s just waiting. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing, and then I got written up last week, so… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't tap the scanner in that certain way or if you moved your hand from the cash drawer without freezing for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not like something bad would happen. I just get stuck. The tapping, or my hand staying over the cash drawer… it doesn’t make much sense, and the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things in a certain order or in a symmetrical way when you're at the register?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… I get stuck in a pattern, like tapping the scanner, or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer when I should be helping the customer. It doesn’t really feel like an order thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about germs or contamination when you're working at the register?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about germs. I mean, I handle money and stuff, but that’s not really what I’m worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't do these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, you would make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. I don’t think, “If I don’t tap, I’ll make a mistake.” It’s more like I just… get caught doing it, or I freeze, and then I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. The customer’s waiting, and last week I got written up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would lead to something going wrong with the transaction?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think something would go wrong with the transaction because I didn’t do it. That’s what got me in trouble last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these actions at the register would lead to you making an error with the customer's order?\n\nPatient: No… not really an error with the order. It’s more that I get stuck doing those things, like tapping the scanner or just freezing by the cash drawer, and then I’m not moving the line along. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would make you feel like you've made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’ve made a mistake, no. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that doing these specific actions at the register helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it helps me avoid mistakes. It’s just something I get stuck doing, like tapping the scanner or stopping over the cash drawer, and then the customer’s waiting. That’s what caused the trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these actions at the register is related to trying to do your job perfectly?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. I just sort of get stuck there, tapping the scanner or holding still by the drawer, even when the customer’s waiting. It doesn’t really fit what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that doing these actions at the register is somehow connected to preventing an error from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it prevents anything. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to tap the scanner in a certain way or freeze with your hand over the cash drawer because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “compelled” is the right word. It’s more like I get stuck doing it… tapping the scanner or just freezing there with my hand over the cash drawer, even though the customer’s waiting and I should move on. It’s been enough of a problem that I got written up last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you get stuck doing these actions at the register?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess distressed, mostly because it’s embarrassing. I’m standing there tapping or frozen over the cash drawer and the customer’s just waiting. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing, and then I got written up last week, so… yeah, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't tap the scanner in that certain way or if you moved your hand from the cash drawer without freezing for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not like something bad would happen. I just get stuck. The tapping, or my hand staying over the cash drawer… it doesn’t make much sense, and the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things in a certain order or in a symmetrical way when you're at the register?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… I get stuck in a pattern, like tapping the scanner, or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer when I should be helping the customer. It doesn’t really feel like an order thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about germs or contamination when you're working at the register?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about germs. I mean, I handle money and stuff, but that’s not really what I’m worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't do these specific actions at the register, like tapping the scanner a certain way, you would make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. I don’t think, “If I don’t tap, I’ll make a mistake.” It’s more like I just… get caught doing it, or I freeze, and then I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. The customer’s waiting, and last week I got written up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would lead to something going wrong with the transaction?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think something would go wrong with the transaction because I didn’t do it. It’s more that I get stuck doing it, or I freeze there with my hand over the cash drawer, and then the customer’s just waiting. That’s what got me in trouble last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these actions at the register would lead to you making an error with the customer's order?\n\nPatient: No… not really an error with the order. It’s more that I get stuck doing those things, like tapping the scanner or just freezing by the cash drawer, and then I’m not moving the line along. That’s what got me written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that not doing these specific actions at the register would make you feel like you've made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’ve made a mistake, no. It’s more… I get caught up in the tapping or I just stop with my hand over the drawer, and then the customer’s waiting. That’s the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that doing these specific actions at the register helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it helps me avoid mistakes. It’s just something I get stuck doing, like tapping the scanner or stopping over the cash drawer, and then the customer’s waiting. That’s what caused the trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these actions at the register is related to trying to do your job perfectly?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. I just sort of get stuck there, tapping the scanner or holding still by the drawer, even when the customer’s waiting. It doesn’t really fit what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that doing these actions at the register is somehow connected to preventing an error from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it prevents anything. I just get stuck doing it, like tapping the scanner or freezing over the cash drawer, and then people are waiting. That’s what got me in trouble at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have more control over the situation when you're doing these specific actions at the register?\n\nPatient: Not really. It doesn’t feel like control. I just… get stuck doing it, like tapping the scanner or holding my hand over the drawer, and the customer’s just standing there waiting. Last week that got me written up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
